#3187. Marvell Agreed to Pay $1,132 a Share for Lumentum. The Stock Trades Near $510. Here's Why It’s Not as Good As It Looks.

The gap between Lumentum's stock price and Marvell's offer looks like easy money. It isn't.

When Marvell Technology (MRVL -16.43%) agreed to acquire Lumentum (LITE -18.68%) on Nov. 2, the deal valued Lumentum at about $1,132 per share, a roughly 24% premium over its pre-talks trading price. Three days later, Lumentum closed at about $510.

That's not quite the bargain it seems. Even based on the current value of Marvell's offer, Lumentum trades at about 85% of what it would be worth if the deal closes, a gap of about 15% (we'll discuss why we say "current value" in a bit). Here's why that gap exists, and why I don't think it's free money.

First of all, Marvell is paying for Lumentum entirely in stock. The acquisition price is well over $100 billion (based on Marvell's share price before the announcement), and acquiring Lumentum for cash simply wouldn't be practical. Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each share they own when the deal closes. There's no collar, a feature some deals use to protect sellers if the buyer's stock falls. So every $1 drop in Marvell's share price cuts $4.50 from the deal's value for Lumentum investors.

Marvell's stock has fallen sharply amid recent market turbulence. It closed at $133.55 on Nov. 5, which puts the offer at about $601 per Lumentum share. That's far less than the $913.95 Lumentum traded for before the deal talks became public. In other words, the premium Lumentum shareholders were promised is gone, at least for now, because it is 100% dependent on Marvell's stock price.

Here's a big reason why there's still a gap, even below the new, lower value of the deal. The companies don't expect to close the deal until the first half of 2028, and the merger agreement allows extensions as late as August 2028 if regulators are still reviewing it. If the deal closes, Lumentum's stock would be worth exactly 4.5 times what Marvell shares are. But spread over that much time, a 15% gain works out to roughly 8% to 13% annualized returns by my estimate, depending on when the deal closes. And the deal closing isn't exactly a sure thing.

Regulators are the biggest wild card. Bank of America downgraded Marvell after the announcement, citing a long review process "that includes China." There's good reason for concern. China's regulator never approved Intel's (INTC -10.33%) planned acquisition of Tower Semiconductor (TSEM -11.97%), which collapsed in 2023. Even deals that do get approved can take a long time. Synopsys' (SNPS -4.89%) acquisition of Ansys took about 18 months to clear.

If the deal falls apart, Marvell would owe Lumentum a $3.2 billion termination fee. That sounds like a lot, but it works out to roughly $35 per Lumentum share, a small cushion for a stock trading above $500.

Professional arbitrage investors typically buy the target and short the acquirer's stock to lock in the spread. And in this case, it might seem like a good idea for your portfolio. After all, 15% is quite a gap on an already beaten-down stock. Most individual investors shouldn't be doing that. Without that hedge, buying Lumentum today is largely a bet on Marvell's stock, with regulatory risk layered on top.

To be fair, Lumentum would be fine on its own. Its revenue grew 137% last quarter, and management expects more growth this quarter. In a nutshell, if the deal breaks, the stock wouldn't necessarily collapse.

Having said all that, if I wanted exposure to this deal, I'd rather own Marvell directly than buy Lumentum and hope the spread closes. The gap between Lumentum's price and the offer isn't a mistake. It's the market's price for time, uncertainty, and Marvell's own volatility.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Intel, Lumentum, Marvell Technology, and Synopsys. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3186. Claude’s new Personal Finance tool aims to help analyze your spending – read-only, of course

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Anthropic has released what appears to be its answer to ChatGPT Finances.

You can now share your financial information with Claude and ask the chatbot for insights on your spending.

Also: Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info

Personal Finance in Claude, which Anthropic announced Thursday, reviews your connected accounts through read-only access via Plaid. That security measure follows OpenAI’s equivalent feature, ChatGPT Finances, which launched in May. Here’s what to know.

Functionally, this feature appears similar to ChatGPT Finances: It lets you link as many bank and credit card accounts as you’d like to Claude via Plaid with read-only access. You can then ask Claude where you spent your money this month or whether you can afford to take a vacation in six months, for example.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Also: Stop sharing your passwords with AI agents

According to Anthropic, the feature is a response to existing use patterns: the company’s April usage report found that financial advice was one of the main topics users ask Claude for help with.

“For the accounts you choose to connect (and only those), Claude sees the bank’s name, each account’s name, type, and last four digits, balances and credit limits, and up to two years of transactions. When you chat with Claude in the Personal Finance hub, those conversations aren’t used to train our models.”

The idea behind the feature is that you can ask follow-up questions and request more insights about spending habits, trends over time, and future goals based on what Claude finds, similar to any other Claude conversation. The tool will also generate tables and charts to visualize your financial information.

“You can ask for a weekly spending chart, a calendar of what’s due, or a dashboard of the numbers you choose, and Claude builds it as an artifact you can keep adjusting and coming back to,” an Anthropic representative told ZDNET via email.

Also: I connected ChatGPT to my bank, and it’s my go-to finance app now

You can also expect an ad-free experience in the feature.

“Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations, and it doesn’t offer credit cards, loans, or savings accounts of its own, so your money questions get a straight answer,” the company said in the announcement.

The company added that because Claude is using actual transaction data and not memory, the feature shouldn’t hallucinate.

“It can list the exact transactions behind any number so you can check its math,” Anthropic said via email. “Like any AI, it can make mistakes, so for anything important, we recommend confirming with your bank.”

Still, the difference — and for many, the appeal — here is the natural language interface. Other finance apps don’t necessarily have an easy, accessible chat function where users can shoot off quick questions like they would to a human financial planner. These assistants also present historical budget analysis in plain English, rather than requiring users to interpret dashboards themselves, which users may see as a perk.

Also: 5 ways to use AI when your budget is tight

On Reddit, many users find ChatGPT Finances to be accurate and reliable. One Reddit user said ChatGPT Finance seemed to have Mint or Monarch potential, but noted it was still “weaker” than those apps in some areas, such as historical syncing, high-quality dashboards, and generating full statement PDFs.

“ChatGPT has genuinely helped me make better financial decisions,” she wrote.

Anthropic noted that users shouldn’t look to Personal Finance for Claude as a total authority. “Personal Finance is a first step. For life’s bigger money decisions, we think people are best served by Claude working alongside the licensed professionals and regulated firms they trust,” the company said in the announcement.

“We’re exploring how to make that possible in Claude, and we’d like to hear from banks, wealth managers, and financial services companies who want to help shape it” — indicating a more integrated financial planning system within Claude may be on the way.

#3185. If the White House Network Can Be Hijacked by Proxies, Your Smart Home Is Already at Risk

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Cybersecurity

A new report finds residential proxies exiting the White House network 236 times in the past month.

As if the Trump administration needed any more bad news this week, cybersecurity researchers discovered several residential proxy services originating from the White House network, exposing a potential security flaw that could allow cybercriminals to route traffic through official government IP addresses.

The security firm Layer3 Intel detected residential proxy activity on AS2650, a network registered to the Executive Office of the President. Another security firm identified software from the proxy server Infatica, a company owned by a Russian entrepreneur who ran a server-rental company used by hackers in attacks on election systems in Arizona and Illinois in 2016.

While there’s no evidence that proxy access was used to carry out attacks on the White House, experts say it could open the door for hackers to exploit vulnerabilities or grant outsiders an unmonitored back door into high-security systems.

“It basically gives them a foothold on a network where previously nobody was getting into the White House network,” Layer3 founder Sergiy Usatyuk told CNET. “From a security policy perspective, it gives you all types of potential pathways to exploit.” (Usatyuk pleaded guilty to federal charges in 2019 for his role in operating websites that launched denial-of-service, or DDoS, attacks.)

Residential proxy services route internet traffic through consumer devices (such as smartphones, laptops or home routers) to make it appear that the activity is being performed by a real person. While proxies can be used for relatively benign purposes — web scraping is by far the most common use case — they’re also popular for activities like malware attacks, identity theft and creating fake social media accounts.

For example, earlier this year, Synthient, another company that tracks proxies and VPNs, documented how one botnet infected more than 2 million devices using a residential proxy server. If a similar vulnerability existed in the White House proxy, hackers could probe the infected device’s internal systems and install malicious payloads.

In its report, Layer3 found traffic from six distinct IP addresses exiting the White House network from at least 28 known residential proxy services between Oct. 5 and Nov. 3, with most activity occurring in the evenings between 5 p.m. and midnight EST. This suggests the culprit may be an employee who connected a personal device to a Wi-Fi network with a compromised app or a proxy SDK (software development kit) running in the background. Third-party SDKs can convert a device into a “proxy node.”

Commercial proxy networks sell access to these nodes, so anyone with a few dollars could rent a trusted IP address — in this case, making it appear as though traffic originates from the Executive Office of the President.

Researchers at Synthient provided more details about the case, posting on X that several White House staffers are running “a Windows 11 machine with InfaticaSDK installed.” They pointed directly to Rave, a popular app that lets users watch streaming services with friends, as the likely delivery mechanism for the residential proxy traffic detected on the White House network.

Riley Kilmer, co-founder of the cybersecurity firm Spur Intelligence, wrote in a LinkedIn post that different types of proxy companies had been discovered in the White House, including one called 9Proxy, which is often used by criminals for banking fraud.

The White House didn’t respond to CNET’s request for comment.

Because smart TVs, connected appliances and home routers usually have minimal user oversight, a single unvetted app or out-of-date firmware update can turn a household of IoT devices into an exit node for commercial proxy networks.

Proxies can generally “glom” onto consumer devices by embedding networking code directly into software, either consensually (users willingly install it) or nonconsensually (by exploiting unsecured device access points).

It starts when app developers look for revenue by integrating those proxy SDKs into their free apps. Then, when you install the app, the fine print of the terms and conditions of service often includes a clause that allows the app to add your IP address to a third-party proxy pool.

A June report from Spur Intelligence found that roughly one in three apps available on Samsung and LG smart TVs were selling users’ IP addresses. To avoid ads, users often accept the terms when downloading the app — a type of unnoticed consent. Once installed, the SDK runs continuously in the background, registering the device’s IP with the proxy provider’s gateway, unless you delete the app or opt out. (The report notes that Amazon and Roku have explicitly banned this practice.)

Using a free VPN could expose you to the same risk, allowing your IP address to be used by someone else for nefarious activities. The FBI’s guidance on protecting yourself against proxies specifically calls out free VPNs as one of the primary ways bad actors gain access to an IP address.

In some cases, you don’t need to download anything to get your IP thrown into a shady proxy pool — you just have to buy the wrong device. A recent investigation by Synthient discovered that more than 1.3 million streaming devices from the China-based device manufacturer MECOOL were infected with proxy SDKs. A proxy service called SocksEscort was charged by the Justice Department in March with compromising thousands of routers to submit fraudulent unemployment insurance claims.

Checking whether your IP address is being used by a proxy service without your knowledge is relatively simple. Layer3 provides a free proxy-checking tool that searches its database of over 70 million proxies to find if yours is listed. GreyNoise, another cybersecurity company, also offers a free IP-checking service.

These tools can tell you if proxy traffic is passing through your network, but they can’t identify which specific device has the SDK running. You’ll have to do some additional detective work to figure out the culprit. Some common sources include free VPNs, gaming apps, “one price, no subscriptions” streaming boxes and smart TVs.

Cybersecurity experts also recommend keeping the firmware on all your internet-connected devices up to date and downloading only from trusted app stores.

#3184. 6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

I’ve been a dedicated Pixel user since the beginning, so I was naturally excited to get my hands on Google’s latest iteration, the Pixel 11 Pro XL. I’ve been impressed with it overall, but I noticed that, as Google has done before, the device isn’t set up perfectly out of the box.

Also: Pixel 11 is $200 off in this early Black Friday deal

After spending a few weeks with the device, I found that several features are either turned off by default or need optimizing to work in the best way possible.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

The camera was the biggest reason I first joined team Pixel back in 2016, so it’s one of the first places I looked on the Pixel 11 Pro XL. New this year is Camera Looks, a setting that changes how images will be processed before you take them. You can change a number of settings, including contrast, shadows, tint, sharpness, and warmth.

Also: A week with the Pixel 11 changed my mind about Gemini

I’ve always loved the camera on any Pixel I’ve had, but I’ll admit that images can look a little over-processed at times. If picture quality is important to you at all, it’s vital to set this feature right out of the box because it applies to every image you take from that point on. You can find Camera Look by opening the camera app, tapping the settings icon in the bottom-right corner, and making your changes.

Pixel’s “At a Glance” lock screen widgets are one of the most sneakily useful features, and you’re missing out if you’re not using them. These widgets are off by default, but when you enable them, you can see real-time information without unlocking your phone. You can see weather info, sports updates, flight information, rideshare and food delivery updates, fitness information, and more.

Also: These tech gifts topped our experts’ 2026 holiday wish lists

To use these widgets, long-press on an open space on your home screen. To customize your widgets, go to Settings > Wallpaper & style > More lock screen settings > Widgets on lock screen. To access your widgets, you just swipe left from your lock screen.

Out of the box, your Pixel 11’s display isn’t set to utilize its full capability. The Pixel 11 series comes with a fantastic low-temperature polycrystalline oxide (LTPO) display, but it’s slightly throttled in the name of battery life. You’ll notice the increased resolution most on text and in fine details. It won’t make a huge difference to your streaming or large display elements, though. If you read consistently, either articles or e-books, you’ll want to make the change.

To change this, head to Settings > Display > Screen Resolution. Bump the setting from “High resolution,” which is 1080 x 2404, to “Max resolution,” which is 1344 x 2992.

If you’re anything like me, your phone usually needs a quick charge some evenings. The Pixel 11 series introduced Extreme Charging Mode, which accelerates charging by pausing background activity and non-essential apps. Google says the mode should charge the Pixel 11 and 11 Pro to over 50% in 30 minutes, and the Pixel 11 Pro XL to 75% in 30 minutes. From my testing, that estimate was pretty accurate.

Also: After testing the Pixel 11, I feel fine about skipping the Pro models

There are a few caveats: this feature is only available when your phone is at or below 50% battery, and you do need to turn it on every time you use it. But after the first time you do it, it’s available in your quick settings. Head to Settings > Battery & charging > Extreme Charging to turn it on the first time.

If you have the newest Pixel, you might as well take advantage of one of its biggest selling points. I felt like this feature was gimmicky at first (and I’m still a little grumpy it replaced my beloved thermometer), but the more I use it, the more I like it. The multicolor LED on the phone’s rear camera bump can indicate who’s calling, serve as a status indicator for Gemini, and more — and it’s almost certain that more usability will arrive over time.

Where I found the most use was when I had my phone on silent. After assigning specific colors to contacts (you can do this from the contact information screen), I could easily see whether a call was worth picking up without reaching for my phone.

To make sure you’re getting the most out of your new device, head in and change your contact HiLight colors right away. It only takes a few seconds and makes deciding whether to answer a call a lot easier.

We’ve all accidentally swiped away an important notification or missed a notification in a sea of updates. The Pixel 11 offers two notification features to help you stay on top of things.

Notification History is a feature that logs every alert from the last 24 hours, including those you swipe away. This is off by default, meaning when you dismiss a notification, it’s gone forever. With this on, you get a searchable, chronological backup.

Also: Google’s big flagship is more Pro than ever

AI Notification Summaries, as the name implies, takes long multi-notification threads and stacked notifications and turns them into bullet points. This feature is powered by on-device Gemini Intelligence, and you can choose which apps to summarize.

You can find both of these under Settings > Notifications > Notification history & Notification Summaries.

#3183. Despite Strong Revenue Growth and a Cheap Valuation, Supermicro Stock Is Still Not a Buy

The data center server specialist has become one of the most divisive stocks in the market.

With a long history of controversy, Super Micro Computer (SMCI -8.51%) is one of the most divisive stocks in the market. The company has been growing revenue quickly and remains a clear beneficiary of the AI build-out. However, it also has a history of accounting issues, and three now-former employees have been charged by the Department of Justice with violating U.S. technology export bans. As such, the stock is down by more than 35% over the past year, while rivals Dell and Hewlett Packard Enterprise have more than doubled over the same period.

Supermicro designs and assembles servers and rack solutions for data centers. It normally customizes its systems around Nvidia's graphics processing units (GPUs), and provides turnkey solutions that include things like networking, cooling, and software. The company has said its big advantage is speed, as its integrated rack solutions get customers up and running more quickly.

Despite being a key player in the artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out, Supermicro has dealt with a lot of issues over the past few years. A short-seller accused the company of accounting irregularities in 2024, and the subsequent investigations eventually led to the company's auditor resigning and delays in its regulatory filings. This was preceded by the SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission) fining the company in 2020 for improperly recognizing revenue, and in 2018, the stock was delisted from the Nasdaq after the company failed to file financial reports. It did not relist on the exchange until 2020.

The biggest issue the company faces currently, however, is that three former employees, including one of its co-founders, have been charged with illegally smuggling high-end Nvidia GPUs into China through an elaborate scheme that involved fake paperwork, "dummy" servers, and a Thailand-based company that acted as an intermediary. Throughout all these scandals, somehow, CEO Charles Liang has managed to stay in charge.

While the company has dealt with a lot of controversy, it has also produced some strong growth this calendar year.

In its fiscal 2027 first quarter (which ended Sept. 30), Supermicro's revenue nearly quadrupled year over year from $5 billion to $19.9 billion. That was well above the $14.5 billion to $15.5 billion range that management forecast in August.

Supermicro is in a low-gross-margin business, as much of its revenue is essentially the pass-through cost of expensive GPUs. It has dealt with issues in the past, but its gross margins appear to be recovering from a recent all-time low. Gross margin came in at 12.7% in fiscal Q1. That was up from 9.3% a year ago, although down from 17.5% last quarter. The company said it is seeing improvement stemming from the growing contribution of higher-margin solutions, like its DCBBS (Data Center Building Block Solutions) model, which is a modular, pre-designed turnkey solution that includes customer-specific software.

Adjusted earnings per share (EPS), meanwhile, soared from $0.35 to $1.81. That was well ahead of its guidance for EPS of between $1.01 and $1.10.

Management projects that fiscal 2027 Q2 revenue will be between $19.5 billion and $21.5 billion, up from $12.7 billion in the prior-year period. It guided for EPS of between $1.66 and $1.90, up from $0.69 last year.

The company also raised its fiscal-year revenue forecast to between $72 billion and $82 billion, up from a prior outlook of $65 billion to $72 billion.

Supermicro is seeing incredible growth, while the stock trades at a cheap forward price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) of just above 6, based on fiscal 2027 analyst estimates. That said, I would still not buy the stock.

The company's checkered history will likely keep a lid on its valuation multiples. Meanwhile, according to Goldman Sachs, Supermicro is highly tied to the neocloud niche, estimating that it has about a 40% share of the server market for neoclouds. The neoclouds already face financing issues relating to the debt they must take on to build out new data centers.

Moreover, after the new crop of governors and legislators elected in this week's midterms takes office, changes across various governments could lead to longer permitting timelines for data centers, create additional hurdles to their construction, and put pressure on the space. That would be bad news for SMCI, and at the least could reduce the value of its speed advantage.

As such, I'd avoid Supermicro --  there are better AI stocks to own.

Geoffrey Seiler has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Goldman Sachs Group, Hewlett Packard Enterprise, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3181. HR thinks you can learn AI skills – so why is your company hiring elsewhere?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Over 90% of HR professionals say they believe employees can learn AI roles - but half of companies still hire outside for those positions, according to a new report.

HR leaders are confident that current employees can be trained for jobs created by artificial intelligence. Organizations are hiring externally instead.

That’s the finding in new research from Careerminds, a global provider of workforce solutions. The study, published in Careerminds’ white paper, The AI Redeployment Gap: What Companies Lose by Hiring over Reskilling, reveals that 92% of HR professionals and 88% of people managers believe employees within their organizations could be reskilled for AI-related roles.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

Yet 51% of HR professionals and 47% of people managers report that their organizations are hiring externally for new AI-related positions, compared with just 42% in each group who report redeploying existing employees.

The research was conducted in partnership with Censuswide and surveyed 595 US HR professionals and 547 US people managers, ages 25–65, working at organizations with 500-10,000 or more employees. The research was conducted May 15–26, 2026.

Also: Businesses finally seeing AI ROI, but 62% can’t handle the storage demands

“Companies are investing in AI while potentially overlooking one of their most valuable resources: the people they already employ,” said Mark Saddic, senior partner at Careerminds, in a press release.

“Our research shows that organizations largely believe their employees can adapt to AI-driven change, but that confidence isn’t consistently translating into opportunities. Before looking outside the organization, leaders need to understand the skills they already have, identify where employees can grow, and establish a clear process for making those transitions possible.”

Reskilling, especially for AI, isn’t easy to track. Limited visibility into individual skill sets and which employees should be reskilled was cited as a potential barrier by 43% of HR professionals and 34% of managers.

Also: Pearson’s Workera deal targets a big workplace problem: No time to AI upskill

It’s not the first time that the management of skills development has come up regarding AI. In September, educational giant Pearson agreed to acquire Workera, the AI-native skills platform used by organizations such as ServiceNow, Accenture, and the US Space Force.

Careermind’s data revealed that 87% of HR pros and 84% of managers believe their organizations are adequately considering reskilling employees for future roles. However, the findings suggest that these organizations may believe they are prioritizing internal talent development, even though their hiring practices reveal otherwise.

Response to AI-related workforce needs isn’t equal across the board. The findings reveal significant differences in how industries are responding to these needs.

Also: Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

For example, in the technology and telecom industries, external hiring outpaces redeployment by 15%. At the same time, media and entertainment is the only sector surveyed in which both groups report redeployment at least as frequently as external hiring. Among HR professionals in that industry, 56% report redeploying employees, compared with just 25% who hire externally.

Separate research from The Josh Bersin Company, cited in the Careerminds white paper, reveals that external hiring can cost three to five times more than filling comparable roles internally — and on average takes up to 20 days longer.

#3180. A Wall-Mounted Garden Frame Is Peak Winter Decor (and a Great Gift)

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I tested Modern Sprout's handsome Growframe for a pop of green during fall and winter.

My pursuit of good houseplant tools means I’m always looking for shops and resources. That led me to Modern Sprout, a Chicago-based online store that sells plants and plant-care accessories. When they reached out offering a chance to test some of its high-design indoor garden tech, I decided to see what, if anything, is worth considering for your houseplant needs or as a place to get gifts for the plant parents in your life. I found a few pleasant surprises.

Whether I’m tending to houseplants in winter or gardening in summer, I’m always looking for tools to improve plant care. For houseplants in particular, this means watering, trimming, fertilizing, occasionally repotting and providing as much real estate in sunny windows as possible. When that’s not enough, I harness the power of full-spectrum LED grow lights, so I can have greenery in practically every room of my apartment.

A quick look at Modern Sprout’s website shows it sells most of what you’d expect from a boutique plant nursery: live plants, potting soil, gardening accessories, grow lights, grow kits, candles, bird feeders and gift sets. While Modern Sprout carries plenty of its own products, it also curates items from other small businesses instead of relying on large mainstream gardening brands. This is a nice bonus for folks who prefer to support smart business owners. Modern Sprout also offers wholesale accounts, so you might even see it at a nursery near you.

For my tests, I stuck to the Modern Sprout brand, which is also available on Amazon. Since I review a lot of smart home gear, I wanted to try out the Smart Landscape Growframe ($229) and compare it with other plant light options I use. I also wanted to see if Modern Sprout’s tools are any good, so I rounded out my experience with three essentials behind effective houseplant care: Pruning Shears ($20), a Glass Watering Can ($48) and Dissolving Plant Food Sheets ($20).

I don’t think anything could’ve prepared me for the size of the Smart Landscape Growframe: 32.1 inches long, 11.5 inches tall and 5.3 inches deep. For some context, that’s wider than my kitchen table and large enough to require a careful survey of my home to find a suitable location. I found a nice spot above my stairs next to the front door.

Additionally, its durable powder-coated steel frame weighs an incredible 8 pounds. That’s roughly the weight of a gallon of milk, which makes wall mounting tricky. I agree with Modern Sprout’s recommendation to place the mounting screws in studs, so you’ll need a stud finder, a level, and a power drill if you want it on the wall. You can use drywall anchors, but they aren’t included.

Make sure you visually line up the Growframe’s mounting holes with your marks before driving screws into the wall. This is easier with a second person, but I managed it alone. However, I relied purely on my measurements and had to redo the screws after one was slightly off. I suggest marking one stud first, then holding the planter and using a level to see where the other should go. Similarly, keep in mind that the 9-foot power cord needs to reach an outlet.

The app isn’t complex and is really easy to use. I really like that there are preset schedules for shade, partial sun and full sun light levels — these options always start at 8 a.m., providing 8, 12 and 16 hours of lighting at different intensities, respectively. The presets aren’t customizable, so you must use one of the app’s two other scheduling options to adjust the timing and other settings. I noticed that toggling a preset works as intended, but closing and reopening the menu shows an inactive toggle — a minor visual bug that doesn’t seem to affect the feature. (It might even get patched by the time you read this review.)

You can also set a schedule using specific times of day in the Growframe device menu. While this particular scheduling menu lacks an option to tie the lighting controls to sunrise and sunset, you can find that on the Scenario tab. (Sunrise/sunset automation isn’t necessary for most tropical plants and herbs people put in the planter, but it’s nice to have a temperate lighting option that changes throughout the year.)

The Scenario tab offers a few automation options based on device status, scheduling and weather changes, plus a way to set up manual Tap-to-Run automations. Beyond that, there isn’t much else to the app, which makes sense and aligns with the company’s goal of keeping things simple. However, folks wanting smart home integrations with a platform like Home Assistant or Samsung SmartThings might be better off using a smart plug with a different grow light brand.

Speaking of grow lights, the full-spectrum unit inside the top of the Smart Landscape Growframe can reach up to 1,480 lumens, which is very bright considering most plants in the planter will be within 6 inches or less. Given the size limit on the plants you can put in the planter, I doubt you’ll have any issues with giving your plants enough light. I want to note that the adhesive holding one of the grow light strips in my sample unit came loose during shipping, but it was easy enough to reattach.

Planting in the Smart Landscape Growframe is fairly easy. It comfortably holds up to seven potted plants (2- to 3-inch pots), though I ended up putting five 4-inch potted herbs in my setup. One pot in particular is a little taller than the planter box lip, but it doesn’t bother me.

Be mindful of pot size in the planter, as rims that press up against the side can cause water to overflow onto the floor, as it did with mine. The best solution is to remove a little soil from overfilled pots and to add a small notch or hole in a side of the pot that drains into the basin.

The potted herbs I got from my local garden center needed a little trimming before I could put them in the Smart Landscape Growframe, so it was a good opportunity to try out the Pruning Shears. I’m a huge fan of the all-metal design, which is easily one of the most comfortable pairs of gardening shears I’ve used. The nifty locking latch at the end of the handle isn’t metal — it doesn’t need to be — but it’s easy to use and doesn’t require any fiddling.

I also love the needle-nose blades, which are easy to maneuver into tight spaces when trimming dead growth off plants — they are great for trimming all of my herbs. Although I’ll admit that the densely packed chives tested my patience, that’s hardly the shears’ fault.

Of the items I tested, the glass watering can was my least favorite. For instance, the borosilicate glass may be more durable than other types of glass, but it’s still glass and still fragile. I’m not sure if I trust my clumsy self with it when I water my plants. Similarly, the 1-quart size is great for folks caring for a handful of plants, but it can’t keep up with the gallon-size watering can that I sometimes empty completely just to water my dwarf lemon tree in a large container.

Another issue I had with the glass watering can was the upper rim. While I like that the flared rim makes it easy to fill from my bathroom sink, it can get in the way when watering the Growframe. This is especially tedious when you need to tilt the watering can nearly vertical to get the last drops out. Fortunately, you can see when the water is almost gone, but that’s the only real advantage it has over my normal watering can.

I often put off fertilizing houseplants because it feels like a hassle. My main reason is that most of the carnivorous plants I grow don’t like fertilizer, so I skip it on other houseplants. (I get around this with plant food spikes, but I’m hardly consistent at that either.) A secondary reason is that keeping the fertilizer out where I’ll use it isn’t the best use of space in my apartment. While Modern Sprout’s Dissolving Plant Food Sheets don’t really solve the former problem, they’re very convenient for the latter.

Just like when I tested dishwasher detergent sheets, I really like how easy it is to toss Modern Sprout’s fertilizer into the watering can. A full sheet treats a gallon and has perforations so you can tear it into segments to fit your watering can. Since the glass watering can holds one quart, I needed only one-quarter of a sheet. The sheets dissolved a little slowly, and I had to stir them for a minute or two before no fragments were left.

I’m a fan of the seaweed sheets, which are a great option for plant food that isn’t entirely a collection of processed chemicals. Despite the seaweed ingredient, the sheets don’t explicitly state they’re certified organic, so keep that in mind if you use them for herbs or microgreens.

All of the Modern Sprout products I tested have a premium design and build quality that makes them easy to recommend for people who value form as much as function. I consider the Pruning Shears the best value in the bunch. They feel incredibly sturdy and capable for $20 and are a huge step up from my $10 Vivosun Gardening Scissors in every way. I haven’t tested Modern Sprout Bypass Hand Pruners, but I suspect they’ll easily rival the Fiskars Shears I use for thicker growth.

While $229 is a lot to ask for the Smart Landscape Growframe, keep in mind that premium heavy-duty steel planters without smart lighting already cost more than those made from other materials. Likewise, more affordable — and more powerful — grow lights are on the market, but Modern Sprout’s intuitive app includes every scheduling and customization feature I consider essential. Even people who don’t use other smart home gear should find it easy to navigate.

I think the Dissolving Plant Food Sheets are a fairly nice option for seaweed-based fertilizers, but the 20-pack will treat only 20 gallons of water at the normal dilution. This makes the sheets generally more expensive per feeding than liquid kelp fertilizers from brands like TPS Nutrients and Grow Queen. That said, Modern Sprout is more convenient to measure, and it won’t cause a liquid or powdery mess if you knock the package over. It’s a great gift, but not necessarily for an enthusiast with a large collection of houseplants.

I wouldn’t buy the Glass Watering Can, but I don’t think it’s a bad product. It’s undeniably attractive, but $48 is more than most. I’ll stick with my cheaper, larger plastic watering can. Overall, Modern Sprout suits folks with higher budgets or as an attractive gift option for your favorite plant nerd.

#3179. Coca-Cola Has Raised Its Dividend for 64 Straight Years. Here's What $10,000 Will Pay You in 2027.

The beverage giant remains one of the stock market's go-to income investments for good reason.

When most investors begin their search for a new income holding, yield is a top-of-mind criterion. And understandably so. More is better.

There's far more to the matter than just a starting yield, though, particularly for long-term investors. Reliability counts too, as does reliable dividend growth. And whether a stock can deliver that is ultimately a function of the underlying business.

With that as the backdrop, beverage behemoth Coca-Cola (KO -0.07%) may well be one of the market's best dividend stocks despite its relatively low forward-looking yield of 2.2%. Here's why.

You know it best for its namesake drink, although it sells so much more than sodas. Minute Maid juices, Dasani water, Powerade sports drinks, and Gold Peak teas are just some of the other brands in Coca-Cola's product portfolio. It's got something for consumers' ever-changing tastes.

Coca-Cola may not be quite the company you think it is, though. While at one point it handled much of its own bottling and distribution, the vast majority of its bottling duties are now handled by third-party companies, some of which it spun off. This allows the company to focus on doing what it does best: marketing.

This model also offloads the bulk of the cost-based risk of being in the beverage business onto the bottlers themselves. That's a big reason Coca-Cola's earnings have remained relatively steady -- and even continued to grow -- in the tough inflationary and macroeconomic environment we've been in since 2021.

KO EPS Diluted (Quarterly) data by YCharts.

The company's revenue mostly just reflects sales of concentrated flavor syrups to these bottlers -- a business with fewer variable costs.

Perhaps more important to interested income investors, it's a rock-solid business model that supports continued payouts. Not only has Coca-Cola paid dividends like clockwork for decades now, but it has raised its per-share payments every year for the past 64 years. There's no end to that streak in sight, either, given the strength of its product portfolio. After all, several of its brands are the leading names in their respective categories.

Still, there's at least one reason would-be buyers might want to keep their powder dry for the time being. That's this ticker's sizable run-up over the course of the past three years. In step with demand for AI-related stocks, many investors have been simultaneously adding safer names like Coca-Cola to their portfolios, quietly hedging against the technology sector's uncertainty and steep valuations.

However, these defensive-minded buyers have arguably overshot their target. After a gain of nearly 70% since this time in 2023, Coca-Cola shares now sport a forward-looking dividend yield of only 2.2%. That's not the worst yield out there. But you can certainly find far better ones, including from companies that are growing their dividend payments at the same pace that Coca-Cola is.

When you're talking about your true long-term holdings, though, you have to think bigger-picture, recognizing that much will change in the future that you simply can't foresee. That's why it's wise to own stakes in companies that are capable of performing no matter what's thrown at them. Coca-Cola has been in a simple business for well over a century, and it's the obvious leader in the space. That's why it may still be worth stepping into at its current price if you aren't comfortable holding out for some measure of a pullback.

And for perspective on what's possible, investing $10,000 in Coca-Cola shares today would generate roughly $220 in dividend income over the year ahead. That's not a huge amount, but in five years' time you won't be worrying too much about whether you got in at its current record high or were able to sneak in at a somewhat better price. Just worry about buying quality. The rest typically takes care of itself.

James Brumley has positions in Coca-Cola. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3177. Rocket Report: Artemis II literally tore through the atmosphere; New Glenn delay

Interstellar Technologies announces more government funding for the Japanese Zero rocket.

Welcome to Edition 9.18 of the Rocket Report! Blue Origin packed a lot of news into one afternoon Thursday with the reveal of a new kick stage for the New Glenn rocket, a new rocket engine design, and the minor and widely expected delay for New Glenn’s return-to-flight mission from December until January. The reconstruction of Blue Origin’s wrecked launch pad at Cape Canaveral, Florida, continues apace after a catastrophic explosion nearly six months ago. There’s a lot of pent-up demand from NASA, Amazon, and the Space Force for Blue Origin to get New Glenn back in action.

SpaceX vets work on water-fed propulsion. Three brothers who worked at SpaceX in the Falcon 9 era are now building satellites that run on water, a potentially harvestable resource on the Moon and found all over the Solar System, Ars reports. In late 2022, the three Carlisle brothers founded Argo Space to build infrastructure in space. Their vision was not just in-space transportation but also removing the need to use propellants from Earth to move around the heavens. Argo Space’s satellites will use water not only as a propellant, but also as a coolant and radiation shield.

How does this work? … Argo Space flew its first mission in July, but the spacecraft failed after one of its solar arrays didn’t deploy, and there wasn’t enough time to test the propulsion system. Two more missions are on Argo Space’s schedule for 2027 to demonstrate the company’s water-based thrusters. The propulsion system is basically a small rocket that uses hydrogen and oxygen as propellants. However, instead of launching with separate tanks of liquid hydrogen and liquid oxygen, the satellite carries a reservoir of liquid water. In space, the vehicle uses solar power to split water into hydrogen and oxygen gases and burns them to generate thrust.

JAXA backs small satellite launcher. Japanese launcher startup Interstellar Technologies has secured the backing of the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA) for its Zero rocket program, Aviation Week & Space Technology reports. The company said JAXA selected it as part of its Acceleration of Commercial Rocket Launch Demonstration program under the Space Strategic Fund. The government-funded program supports development and flight demonstrations of private launch companies with grants of up to $76 million per company, with the goal of fostering a growing commercial launch industry in Japan. Interstellar previously raised more than $250 million, with roughly equivalent funds from government and private capital.

Zeroing in … Interstellar is using the money to complete development of its Zero rocket, a two-stage launcher designed to carry up to 1 metric ton (2,200 pounds) of payload to low-Earth orbit. “We have achieved critical technical milestones, including Japan’s first successful firing test of an orbital-class methane engine,” said Satoshi Nakayama, director of the board and president of Interstellar. “We have now reached the stage where we must swiftly turn these proven development results into operational commercial services.” The most recent update from Interstellar indicated a first launch of Zero in 2027.

Launch demand outpacing supply. A new report from analytics firm Quilty Space confirms that, outside of China, the world faces a significant shortage of rockets over the next five years, Ars reports. The firm estimates that between now and 2030, there will be a shortfall of about 150 launches compared to demand from satellite operators and civil space agencies. If demand from the US military is added in—and SpaceX does not extend the Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy program to accommodate increased national security needs—the shortage could be doubled.

Worse than we thought … “What we wanted to get at is to understand how severe this launch crisis is and when will it be resolved,” said Caleb Henry, director of research at Quilty Space. “What we learned is that the launch crisis is more severe than we thought and that it will not go away this decade for sure, and it might not abate until sometime late in the 2030s.” Why is this happening? Quilty puts the blame on several factors: the war in Ukraine taking Russian rockets off the Western market; Amazon Leo demand; developmental delays in a new generation of rockets, from New Glenn and Vulcan to Neutron, Terran R, and Nova; and more recently, the retirement of the Falcon 9 as a rocket for commercial service.

China continues launching mega-constellation. Another 18 satellites for China’s SpaceSail Internet network launched Tuesday (Monday, US time) from a spaceport in the country’s northwestern desert. The mission marked the second launch—and first operational flight—of the Kinetica 2 rocket developed by a Chinese startup known as CAS Space. The SpaceSail constellation, also known as Qianfan, now consists of some 275 flat-panel satellites from a series of launches that began in August 2024. The network’s first phase will number 1,296 satellites.

A mini-Falcon Heavy … The Kinetica 2 is an interesting launch vehicle, with three side-by-side boosters arranged in a manner similar to SpaceX’s Falcon Heavy. But the Kinetica 2 is a much smaller rocket, with three kerosene-fueled engines on each booster instead of nine. Its lift performance to low-Earth orbit is about 12 metric tons (26,000 pounds). CAS Space plans to eventually recover and reuse the Kinetica 2 boosters.

India launches strategic navigation satellite. A Geosynchronous Satellite Launch Vehicle (GSLV) rocket launched October 30 with a satellite to restore India’s regional navigation system, NDTV reports. The NVS-03 satellite is the third spacecraft in India’s second-generation navigation network. Some of the fleet’s older satellites suffered from atomic clock failures, rendering them partially or totally unusable. NVS-03 carries multiple clocks of different designs in an attempt to overcome the system’s reliability problems.

A “naughty boy” … India’s medium-lift GSLV has now launched 20 times since debuting in 2001. The rocket doesn’t fly as often as India’s smaller launcher, the PSLV, and hasn’t always performed well when it has. Six of the 20 flights ended in total or partial failures, but most of the mishaps occurred early in the rocket’s history. GSLV’s track record earned it the title “naughty boy” from Indian space officials. The most recent launch was a different story. The head of India’s space agency, V. Narayanan, declared the GSLV’s recent flight was the “most precise launch of any rocket in the world.” We’re not sure what metric Narayanan used to make that claim, but the launch was, indeed, a success.

China reveals roadmap to lunar landing. Chinese space officials confirmed that the Asian nation plans to land its astronauts on the Moon within three years, Ars reports. At the Seventh Manned Spaceflight Conference in Beijing, officials, including Wang Xiang, chief designer for the lunar exploration program, laid out the country’s most publicly detailed plans yet for a series of missions leading up to a crewed lunar landing “before 2030.” China’s lunar landings will involve a lunar-orbit rendezvous similar to what NASA did in the 1960s and early 1970s with the Apollo program. However, lacking a rocket as powerful as the Saturn V vehicle, the crew and lunar lander will launch separately to the Moon.

These are the rockets … China’s Long March 10 rocket family will be the workhorse for the country’s initial crew missions to the Moon. The next big test will be the demonstration flight of a Long March 10A rocket, which flies with a single core booster, and China’s new Mengzhou Moon ship to low-Earth orbit in early 2027. The larger Long March 10, with three boosters connected together, will launch China’s Lanyue lunar lander on an uncrewed test flight to the Moon in 2028. The next year, a follow-up mission will involve the concurrent launch of two Long March 10s, one with an uncrewed Mengzhou spacecraft and the other with a Lanyue lander that will dock together in lunar orbit. Then, before 2030, another dual launch of Long March 10s will occur, this time with crew on the Mengzhou spacecraft, leading to the crewed landing mission.

Blue Origin’s big reveals. Blue Origin announced two significant upgrades to its New Glenn rocket on Thursday, reducing the cost of manufacturing the vehicle and expanding its capacity for payload delivery to geostationary orbit and the Moon, Ars reports. With these upgrades, the company aims to eventually position New Glenn to launch more than 1,000 times a year. One of the announcements involved a new engine, called Northstar, that will eventually replace the BE-4 engine that powers the first stage of New Glenn. The other piece of news was that Blue Origin is developing a hydrogen-oxygen third stage for the rocket to give it an extra kick for high-energy missions.

Blue’s Northstar … The Northstar engine is a simplified but more powerful version of the BE-4 engine. It has half the number of parts and 30 percent less mass, and about 65 percent can be 3D-printed, all while providing a thrust increase over the BE-4. Blue Origin believes it can manufacture the engine at 25 percent the cost of a BE-4 engine. The kick stage is somewhat of an engineering marvel in itself. The stage will use a lightweight composite fuel tank, but officials were not sure the structure could contain leak-prone hydrogen at high pressure. The company recently had a breakthrough at Launch Site One in Texas, when it completed its first composite liquid hydrogen tanking test, giving engineers confidence to move forward with the design.

New Glenn won’t return to flight this year. Blue Origin now plans to resume launches of its New Glenn rocket in January, delaying the much-anticipated flight of the vehicle after a destructive launch pad explosion in May, Bloomberg reports. The Jeff Bezos-backed company had been driving to launch New Glenn again before the end of the year in its quest to challenge SpaceX. But Blue Origin Chief Executive Officer Dave Limp told employees on Wednesday that they are now targeting a flight early in 2027.

What’s a month? … The slight delay is unsurprising, and a January launch would still put Blue Origin well head of initial fears of how long it would take to rebuild its launch pad in Florida. New Glenn has a busy launch manifest after the return-to-flight mission, including a crucial launch in the spring of Blue Origin’s Blue Moon Mark 1 lander, an uncrewed precursor to the company’s future human-rated lander for NASA’s Artemis missions.

Artemis II tore through the atmosphere. The launch of NASA’s Artemis II mission in April caused significant disturbances in Earth’s ionosphere that could have triggered temporary, localized errors in GPS positioning, a new study has found, Space.com reports. The study by a team of researchers from National Cheng Kung University in Taiwan analyzed data from high-precision networks of fixed, ground-based GPS receivers. The researchers used the GPS measurements to reconstruct how the passage of the Artemis II mission’s Space Launch System rocket changed the concentrations of free electrons in the upper atmosphere.

Disturbing the peace … The measurements found that the ionospheric disturbances resulting from the Artemis II launch were more significant than any caused by other rocket launches to date. Curiously, they were up to 10 times more intense than those observed following the test launches of SpaceX’s Starship, which is twice as powerful as the SLS based on liftoff thrust.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#3176. You Should Update to iOS 27.2 Now to Patch Hundreds of Bugs

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems

The update fixes some bugs that could expose your sensitive iPhone data or worse.

Apple released iOS 27.2 on Monday, Oct. 26, just a few days after the launch of the iPhone Duo. While iOS 27.2 brings Siri AI to more languages and updates a few other features, it also patches 141 bugs and security issues, including some that could allow a third party to access data or run commands on your iPhone or leave your device inoperable.

You can download iOS 27.2 now by going to Settings > General > Software Update > Update Now and following the prompts on your screen.

According to Apple’s release notes, iOS 27.2 patches dozens of issues across your iPhone. Some of these issues can cause some serious problems for you and your device.

Nine of these bugs address issues that could lead to kernel corruption on your device. The kernel is the core of your device’s operating system, and kernel corruption can cause your device to freeze or crash. The blue screen of death, for example, could be caused by kernel corruption.

Apple also wrote that seven of these bugs could lead to arbitrary code execution. That means the bug could allow a third party to steal your data or use your device for other nefarious purposes.

The update addresses a lot of other bugs, including one that you might run into if you plug a malicious USB into your device. Apple didn’t write that any of these bugs were being actively exploited, but you should still update to iOS 27.2 now to give yourself the most protection.

For as many bugs as 27.2 fixes, it’s actually a slight decrease from earlier updates. Last month’s iOS 27 update patched even more bugs at 157. The number of bug fixes in both iOS updates are unusual based on data over the past few years. In iOS 26, the most bug fixes included in an update was 88 with iOS 26.6. The update with the next highest number of patches was iOS 18.4 with 75.

Apple didn’t say why there’s been an uptick in the number of bug fixes in iOS updates. Artificial intelligence has been used recently to find and address huge numbers of software bugs; however, that doesn’t appear to be the cause behind the surge in iOS 27.2 or iOS 27. Apple usually notes when a bug is found with the help of AI, yet OpenAI is mentioned four times in iOS 27.2’s release notes, and Claude’s Anthropic is only mentioned thrice.

Apple didn’t immediately respond to my request to comment.

For more iOS news, here’s what you should know about iOS 27.2 and iOS 27.1. You can also check out our iOS 27 cheat sheet.

#3173. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 3: Still Moving Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased again today, giving buyers modest relief from rates above 7%.

Mortgage rates are continuing to cool off as we head into November.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 6.93% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

A significant drop is eye-catching, but bear in mind that you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. Since September, mortgage rates have been hovering around 7%. That’s a full percentage point higher than they were at the beginning of 2026.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.43% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

#3171. Own Target Stock by Nov. 9 to Qualify for Its Dec. 1 Dividend. Here's How Many Shares of the Dividend King You'd Need for $2,000 in Yearly Dividends.

Another big quarterly dividend is coming from one of this year's hottest retailer stocks.

Time is running out to get your hands on Target's (TGT -3.16%) next distribution. The quarterly dividend of $1.16 per share isn't payable until Dec. 1, but you have to be a shareholder by the close of Nov. 9 to be on the receiving end of the discount retailer's latest payout.

Obviously, you should never buy a stock just for a dividend. It's a zero-sum game, as the stock should theoretically adjust $1.16 lower at the open on Nov. 10 when it goes ex-dividend. Never rush your due diligence. With that out of the way, let's get into how many shares you would actually need to lock in $2,000 in passive income.

Despite the "cheap chic" chain's stellar gains -- Target stock is up about 62% this year -- the department store retailer yields a healthy 2.8%. With its triple-digit price tag, it probably takes fewer shares than you think to collect $2,000 in distributions through the next four quarters.

At an annualized dividend of $4.64 per share, it would take 431 Target shares to receive roughly $2,000 in distributions in the year ahead. Now for some even better news: Target shareholders will probably need fewer shares in the future.

Target is a Dividend King, a regal distinction reserved for the dozens of stocks that have boosted their distributions for at least 50 consecutive years. Target's streak of hikes is 55 years long, surviving at least eight recessions.

With the retailer recently boosting its earnings guidance, it should stretch that run to 56 years. As the dividend increases, the number of shares required to reach $2,000 in annual Target dividends should decrease gradually over time. The clock may be ticking, but time is ultimately on your side.

#3169. The Cybertruck is dead, makes way for Cybercab says Elon Musk

Tesla wanted to unseat the Ford F-150, instead it made the Edsel look successful.

Elon’s Edsel is no more. Last night Tesla CEO Elon Musk revealed on social media that his car company is calling time on its failed attempt to wrest control of the American pickup truck market. The Cybertruck will cease production in the middle of 2027, liberating that space in Tesla’s cavernous factory for other uses—more Cybercabs, most likely.

Musk first revealed the Cybertruck in late 2019, back when Tesla was still trading at a theoretically understandable price-to-earning ratio. The pickup’s unusual angular shape owed more to low-res video game models than any concessions to practicality. At the time Musk made fanciful claims that the truck would use a stainless steel monocoque construction, telling his audience that “the body and bed don’t do anything useful” on a normal truck. While some race cars use carbon-fiber monocoques for strength and lightness, making a pickup truck with a stainless steel monocoque chassis would only make sense if someone had an awful lot of stainless steel lying around and needed something to do with it all.

The truck was supposed to go on sale for less than $40,000, and Tesla made its usual stratospheric sales predictions that (had they been accurate) the bare metal EVs would be as ubiquitous as the Model 3 and Y.

But development of the Cybertruck was more troubled than usual for Tesla, which struggled heavily to bring both the Model 3 and Y into mass production. Musk demanded “Lego-like precision” from the workers assembling the trucks, but achieving the 10-micron tolerances required by the Danish toy company was not evidently successful, based on what we saw when we experienced our first Cybertruck in reality.

That was in 2023, when Cybertruck headlines were mostly about recalls and stop sales. By then, the truck used a conventional unibody chassis—conventional for Tesla, not for full-size pickup trucks. Toward the end of that year, Musk told his audience that “we dug our grave” with the truck, perhaps the first public acknowledgment that annual sales of a quarter of a million Cybertrucks probably wasn’t going to happen after all.

Sales never took off, and the divisive shape of the Cybertruck became an all-too visible reminder of the CEO’s role in reshaping the federal government to his liking. Even selling thousands of Cybertrucks to SpaceX couldn’t save the pickup. Now, with America having vociferously rejected Musk’s vision of governance, those wishing to drive something that reminds everyone around them of that vision will only have a few months more to purchase one.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Just keep in mind that booking through Citi Travel may mean giving up hotel loyalty program benefits you would receive by booking directly. #3159. How To Build Real AI Sovereignty

Antoni Kozelski, CEO/founder of Vstorm, an agentic AI engineering firm helping companies implement agentic AI to transform their businesses.

​Gartner forecasts that worldwide sovereign cloud spending will reach $80 billion in 2026, growing more than 35% from 2025. And yet the European Commission reports that the EU still depends on non-EU providers for over 80% of its key digital products, services and infrastructure. Money is pouring into the word “sovereign.” The dependence it is meant to fix has barely moved.

In my own work, I’ve found the reason is rarely the data center. It is ownership: which layers of the stack a company actually holds, which it rents and who can revoke them. Nearly every client who asks us for a “sovereign” system means something different by the word.

The instinct to treat sovereignty as a location (e.g., “Our data sits in-country, and therefore, we are sovereign.”) is the first and most expensive mistake leaders can make. Location is not control. The CLOUD Act lets U.S. authorities compel a U.S.-incorporated provider to hand over data held anywhere in the world, including in an EU data center. The postcode on the server changes nothing. What governs the system is the law that governs the company operating it.

Jurisdiction follows the company, not the data center. Data residency asks where information sits. AI sovereignty asks who holds legal and operational authority over it and whether a foreign entity could alter, disable or disclose it without consent.

No one has finished writing the rules yet. The EU’s Cloud and AI Development Act is still moving through negotiation, its sovereignty framework unsettled. Open standards for agentic systems are still being drafted; the Agentic AI Foundation, hosted by the Linux Foundation and launched in late 2025, exists to coordinate those standards, and my company contributes to that work.

When the standards are still forming, every architecture decision is a sovereignty decision, whether a team recognizes it or not.

I have worked with many organizations that believed they had bought sovereignty, but in reality, they had not. The failure modes I’ve observed are strikingly similar each time:

1. They treat data residency as sovereignty. A local region and a national label are often mistaken for control, but if the control plane, the billing or the support team answers to a foreign parent, the data is still reachable. This is what the industry now calls sovereignty washing.

2. They rent the intelligence layer without noticing. A company can own its prompts, its data and even its process logic and still not own the codebase the system runs on, paying indefinitely for the one layer that decides what the system is allowed to do.

3. They confuse model access with ownership. The models are available to everyone. Calling a closed model through an API is not the same as holding open weights you can run, move or swap on infrastructure you control.

4. They leave the exit untested. The real proof of ownership is whether you can leave. If taking your data and your logic to another provider means a rebuild, you did not own them; you were renting them all along.

Sovereignty is a spectrum, not a switch, and it’s a mistake to chase all of it at once. No single company controls the whole stack—the chips and the critical minerals beneath them are transnational and out of anyone’s sole reach. Real returns come from sequencing, not scale: Own the layers that would hurt most to lose, prove you can exit and then decide what to rent deliberately.

The order is simple: Start with the layers whose loss would cost the most: your data and the process logic that encodes how you run. Next, prove you can move before you scale. Export the data, swap the model and change the provider without re-architecting. Finally, own the deepest layers you reasonably can and rent the rest as a choice, not a default.​

Across the sovereign systems that hold up, I’ve seen three common traits:

1. They are portable. Providers, models and infrastructure can change without a rebuild, but portability is the working test of agentic AI without vendor lock-in.

2. Jurisdiction sits with an entity the owner controls, not a foreign parent that can be compelled from outside.

3. The code and the process logic are held, not licensed indefinitely.

The cost of getting this wrong is now measurable. The Commission’s own target is to cut “EU reliance on non-EU technology providers from more than 80 percent today to 40 percent by 2030,” an admission of how deep the exposure runs. Under the EU AI Act, data-governance failures on high-risk systems carry fines up to €35 million or up to 7% of annual worldwide turnover. And there is a physical floor: The IEA projects data centers will draw around 1,000 TWh by 2030, roughly Japan’s entire electricity consumption. A system you cannot power is sovereign in name only.

The “sovereign” label is a commodity. What decides true sovereignty is which layers you actually own. Again, sovereignty is not the postcode on the server. It is the deed to the house.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Mortgage rates jumped after stronger-than-expected jobs data raised expectations for a Fed rate hike next month.

Mortgage rates are higher today on the heels of strong labor market data released yesterday.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday but 25 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates are always on the move, so if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility.

As for today’s jump: Job openings hit a four-month high of 7.5 million in September, according to the Labor Department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey released yesterday. Hiring picked up, too, while layoffs declined. Friday’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other.

To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.
