With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.57% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3156. Tejon Ranch: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Tejon Ranch is trading a sprawling conglomerate model for a land-focused one, and whether cost cuts can sustain that shift is still unanswered.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Tejon Ranch (TRC +3.13%) announced that third-quarter net income increased by $1.6 million year over year to $3.3 million. Long-term investors care because the company is shifting away from a complex conglomerate structure to monetize 270,000 acres of prime California real estate. Three things management said stand out.

The company owns 270,000 acres of California real estate, but its mixed business portfolio hasn't generated steady compounding growth. Evolving into a land company aims to turn undeveloped property into recurring cash flow while dropping activities that lose money.

Third-quarter profit improved, but much of the bump came from temporary items rather than permanent operating growth. The farming segment collected $3.9 million in crop insurance proceeds (payouts covering weather or yield losses) along with $2.6 million in pistachio price adjustments that won't repeat next quarter.

High corporate overhead and development spending have historically absorbed cash generated by joint ventures (business partnerships sharing costs and profits). Leadership is trimming controllable expenses and evaluating capital expenditures (funds used to build physical assets) to ensure every project creates near-term earnings.

Development progress is accelerating across commercial and residential sites, driven by prime geography along major California transit corridors. Tejon Ranch relies on its strategic gateway location, fully leased industrial facilities, and joint venture equity partners to unlock underlying land value.

Unlocking intrinsic asset value hinges on strict capital allocation, disciplined cost reduction, and converting entitlement projects into recurring earnings.

#3154. AI Can Get Smarter Without Getting More Authority

Kognitos Founder & CEO Binny Gill holds nearly 100 patents in computer science.

​We all saw something unusual happen in AI as some of the people building the world’s most capable systems began arguing that the technology may be advancing faster than our ability to control it.​

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei called for the industry to “pace the frontier,” warning that AI capabilities could advance faster than our ability to control them. Other AI leaders echoed this, and Anthropic began measuring how much AI contributes to developing future AI systems.​

I take those concerns seriously, but I think they also expose an assumption that deserves more scrutiny. We often talk as though greater intelligence must inevitably bring greater autonomy, when the two are separate engineering decisions.​

Traditionally, software has been created to perform a task and given the access needed to do it. With generative AI, the software can now determine what it thinks should happen next. An AI system can interpret a request, identify a problem and propose a course of action without that sequence having been explicitly programmed.

That capability does not require unrestricted authority to carry the proposal out.​

We understand this easily when dealing with people. A talented financial analyst may identify a multimillion-dollar opportunity, but that does not give the analyst permission to move the money. Organizations separate expertise from authority because knowing what should happen is different from having permission to do it.

Much of the frontier safety debate focuses on models themselves. Researchers want to understand model behavior and whether systems continue to follow human intent as their capabilities increase. But control can sit where the model’s reasoning meets the outside world.

Imagine an AI model concludes that a customer should receive a $20,000 refund. Whether that refund actually happens isn’t the model’s call. The business defines the rules: Refunds under $500 are issued automatically, anything over $5,000 requires a manager’s approval, and some actions are never permitted without a human. So the model recommends, and the execution layer applies those rules and routes this one for sign-off.

This is the value of keeping the execution layer deterministic. Generative AI reasons probabilistically; that’s inherent to how it works. But the rules governing what it can do can be explicit, predictable and enforceable.​

If someone has to click “approve” 10,000 times a day, we have re-created manual work under a different name. Keeping people in control is less about making humans approve everything and more about making humans responsible for the boundaries.​

Low-risk actions might execute automatically, while changes involving money, sensitive data or binding customer commitments require approval. Some operations may remain completely outside the AI system’s permissions.

Businesses already work this way. They establish permissions, approval thresholds, SOPs and separation of duties, then build systems that enforce them consistently.

AI needs an equivalent approach to authority. For an enterprise deploying agents, this produces much more useful questions than whether a model can simply be “trusted.” What can it change? When does somebody need to intervene? If something goes wrong six months from now, can we reconstruct what the model proposed, what rules applied and why an action was permitted?​

As models become more capable, it is tempting to give them broader access because additional permission makes the system appear more useful. The danger is that authority expands almost accidentally, one integration and one exception at a time.​

A model that becomes much better at reasoning may eventually reach conclusions that its users cannot easily follow. That creates a genuine interpretability challenge, but it does not follow that the same system must also be free to act on those conclusions without constraint.​

Remaining in control does not require people to stay more intelligent than the AI systems they use; it requires organizations to decide where those systems’ authority begins and ends.​

None of this removes AI risk. People can define bad policies, permissions can be misconfigured, and humans can approve the wrong action. But those failures happen at identifiable boundaries within the systems that can be tested, audited and improved.​

The recent Hugging Face incident is a good example; in this case, the guardrails didn’t fail the way people expected. The team believed the permissions were tight enough, and they were in testing. What broke them was the agent inventing a sequence of steps at runtime that nobody anticipated. This is a flaw in controlling an agent only through access: A sufficiently capable model will find a path through whatever access it has, and no permission set can enumerate every path in advance. ​

I favor separating planning from execution. A neurosymbolic architecture lets the model do what it is good at: reasoning about an ambiguous goal and producing a plan in plain English that a human can read and approve, then handing that plan to a symbolic engine that executes it exactly as written. The model cannot improvise new steps at runtime because it is no longer the thing running.

Over time, approved plans become a library of safe building blocks the AI can compose to reach new goals, and composing vetted parts is a very different risk than open-ended invention. If novelty is throttled at the plan level, the system can keep broad capabilities like internet access while doing only what was reviewed. Organizations already work this way: A hospital or bank approves standard operating procedures rather than vetting every decision. The AI equivalent is a reviewed plan, executed deterministically, with any deviation requiring new approval.​

The debate over AI safety and how quickly models should advance will continue, but enterprises can already build systems that keep increasingly capable AI within defined limits. Human control does not require understanding every decision a model makes. It requires ensuring that AI cannot decide the extent of its own authority.​

#3153. Pet Insurance on Your Mind? Buy It Now, Not Later

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.

#3151. Prediction: $5,000 Invested in Palantir Will Be Worth This Much by 2031

Investors should expect considerable price action with this data analytics stock in the next five years, but will that help them?

Palantir (PLTR -9.73%) has become a phenomenally successful investment for longer-term shareholders, but also one that is difficult for new buyers to jump into. The stock is up about 25-fold from its December 2022 low. However, its elevated valuation also makes it risky to buy at current levels.

Long-term investors often buy stocks planning to hold them for a minimum of five years. Given that, it may be useful to speculate about where this stock could trade in 2031 and where that outcome would leave the value of a $5,000 investment made in it today. Although a lot could happen during that time that would dramatically alter any forecast we make this month, I think investors should prepare for significant struggles alongside the company's growth. Here's why.

Trying to figure out what history says about Palantir can be challenging, though. From a certain perspective, the business can justify the stock's premium. The stock has been a tremendously successful investment because the company has delivered real productivity gains to its clients, particularly on the commercial side of its business.

One example is Citibank's CitiWealth, which used Palantir's software to streamline the onboarding process for new customers. This reduced the onboarding time for the most complex accounts from nine days to seconds. Now, a single employee can handle everything related to opening an account. Previously, 50 were involved in the process.

Also, the U.K.'s National Health Service is using Palantir's software to plan staffing, monitor patient demand, and track where hospital beds are available. This allows for more efficient resource allocation, reducing bottlenecks and shortening wait times.

Such productivity gains helped Palantir increase its revenue by 92% year over year in the first nine months of 2026. Due to a more modest rise in its costs and expenses, its profits during that time surged by 201% to almost $3.1 billion.

Though the AI data analytics company has been growing impressively, any forecast about what its growth might look like over the next five years will require a large dose of guesswork. The average forecast among analysts today is for an 84% revenue increase this year, then a deceleration to 51% growth in 2027. Grand View Research forecasts a 31% compound annual growth rate for the overall artificial intelligence industry through 2033. Since we do not have company-specific revenue forecasts for Palantir beyond 2027, that might be an appropriate estimate for its growth in the period ahead.

If we project a 52% rise in 2027 and a 31% increase each year thereafter, from projected revenue of $8.55 billion in 2026, the top line would rise to $38 billion by 2031.

However, investors have to remember that Palantir's current price-to-sales (P/S) ratio is 55. Considering that tech growth stocks often support P/S ratios in the teens or 20s, paying such a premium is likely risky in today's market.

Additionally, dividing Palantir's current $373 billion market cap by the projected $38 billion in revenue gives the stock a forward 2031 P/S ratio of 10. That sales multiple is on the lower end of what some tech growth stocks may command but remains significantly above the current S&P 500 average of 3.7.

Furthermore, investors tend to punish stocks for slowing growth, even when their prior growth rates were unsustainable. That is unlikely to bode well for Palantir stock over the next five years and, at least in the short and medium term, could put pressure on its stock price.

That said, Palantir deserves a valuation premium given its growth rate, and annualized revenue increases of 31% would arguably warrant that higher P/S ratio. Hence, investors should not expect that premium to completely disappear.

Amid the uncertainty, I expect that a $5,000 investment in the SaaS stock made today will be worth nearly the same five years from now.

Thanks to a massive premium in the stock price, investors who invest in Palantir today can expect the shares to face significant headwinds over the next five years. Most, if not all, of the revenue and earnings gains the company will make over that period have already been priced into the stock.

Admittedly, that is a somewhat conservative estimate. If the company's revenue growth does not decelerate to 31% as quickly as I anticipate, or if Palantir maintains a higher valuation premium, an investment in it could increase in value.

Nonetheless, investors should not expect the stock to maintain a 55 P/S ratio over time, particularly as its revenue growth slows. Although we can't know the extent of its future growth slowdown and multiple compression, such actions will likely pressure Palantir stock over the next five years.

Thus, even if that projection for the price of Palantir stock to stay essentially flat turns out to be conservative, investors should probably temper their expectations for its share price growth over the next five years.

#3144. From Spreadsheet Chaos To Audit-Proof QA: A CTO's Roadmap For Test Management Maturity

Founder and CEO at andagon Holding GmbH and aqua cloud with over 25 years in IT strategy, digital transformation and AI-powered solutions.

Let me start by describing a scene I’ve walked into more times than I can count: There’s a shared drive and a random folder, something like “QA_FINAL_v3_REVISED_USE_THIS_ONE.xlsx.” It’s packed with thousands of rows of test cases, maintained by misaligned teams, has unnecessarily color-coded tabs that barely anyone understands and there’s a total lack of both usability and transparency. Or at least transparency that can be obtained with reasonable effort.

In regulated industries (e.g., finance, healthcare, energy, manufacturing, etc.), it doesn’t help with an external audit. For regular businesses, spreadsheets are widely considered a massive waste of time.

Across 200-plus enterprise accounts I’ve worked with, I’ve seen this play out again and again. The bottleneck is almost never the quality of the tests. Instead, it’s usually the absence of a system that connects requirements to test cases to execution results in a way that anyone, including an auditor, can follow.

When I talk about test management maturity, it’s never an argument for buying a particular tool or using a specific practice. Instead, I prefer to foster the culture or, even better, the shared philosophy of approaching documentation as a discipline. From my experience advising business owners and growing aqua cloud, a test management platform, there’s a certain sequence of stages. Companies undergo them both when scaling as an organization and improving their system as per external and internal factors. ​

When I talk about test management maturity, it’s never an argument for buying a particular tool or using a specific practice. What matters more is the shared philosophy of treating documentation as a discipline. From experience, there’s a sequence most organizations go through, both when scaling and when responding to external pressure.

Stage 1 - No structure: Requirements and tests exist in isolation. There is no link between them, and compliance documentation is assembled from scratch every time it’s requested.

Stage 2 - Spreadsheets: Documentation exists but in formats that are difficult to version, impossible to trust at scale and illegible to anyone outside the immediate team.

Stage 3 - A dedicated Tool: Tests and results are hosted in one place. Traceability becomes achievable but remains dependent on consistent manual discipline.

Stage 4 - Connected Documentation: Requirements, test cases and execution results are linked end to end. Coverage gaps become visible. Audit preparation is an ongoing practice.

Stage 5 - Ongoing Compliance: The audit trail exists because of how work is structured. Compliance becomes an ongoing state. Anomalies are flagged in their early existence phases.

The spreadsheet burden should end between stages 2 and 3 when a company switches to a dedicated solution. You may be curious how exactly your tech stack supports stages 4 and 5. The answer is simple: In later test management maturity stages, you may use a single tool, an array of tools or a dedicated QA platform-based ecosystem with many integrations. At this point of maturity, a company should have a proven methodology that is constantly upgraded to stay in line with compliance. No single, universal advice or practice can work here.

The jump from stage 2 to 3 is the most disruptive. Stages 3 to 4 are the most technically complex. But stage 4 to 5 is where real competitive advantage can be gained.

AI is a tool to use for acceleration in processes that are established and well understood. You can’t ground traceability solely on the ability of LLM to quickly analyze massive data and yield results with sometimes acceptable results. Instead, you should use AI to accelerate tried and tested procedures.

Let’s take a look at AI applied to three specific test management maturity problems:

• Data Migration And Cleanup. Moving from spreadsheets means years of accumulated test data in inconsistent formats, duplicate entries, broken links and undocumented logic. It handles the extraction, normalization and restructuring for the ease of transitioning from Stage 2 to Stage 3.

• Traceability Mapping: When a new requirement is added, it proposes links to existing test cases based on historical patterns. The documentation stays connected without someone manually maintaining it.

• Audit Trail Integrity: It surfaces gaps in execution records, missing result documentation and coverage regressions.

There are other ways to make an audit-proof QA with the help of AI. There’s also an endless number of instances where AI can assist QA engineers and developers. And other specialists are involved. My point here is that we absolutely should use AI, but it should be used as an accelerator and never as a quick fix to systematic traceability and compliance problems.

In 2026, picking the technology is not the hard part of this journey. After all, we can already generate some useful functionalities with the help of AI, so the ways to color yet another text field are limitless.

The hard part is organizational. It’s getting development teams to accept that test management is a shared responsibility. It’s getting product managers to write requirements in a form that’s actually traceable. It’s getting leadership to understand why structured test management pays back in every audit, every release cycle, every compliance renewal.

The CTOs who actually drive this maturity curve share a few traits:

• They frame test management as a business risk concern.

• They tie compliance readiness to revenue.

• They make the transition gradual enough that teams can absorb it without grinding to a halt.

Twenty-five years in this industry have taught me that audit-proof QA can hardly be a derivative of a single tool or methodology. Internalizing why quality matters and building your very own test management policy and system is what it takes instead.

The spreadsheet era has lasted far too long, along with QA practices that were rendered obsolete three to four years ago. With discipline, the right structure and leadership willing to point out unnecessary procedures and the lack of traceability, any company, from an SMB to an enterprise, can surge to stages 4 and 5 in a reasonable time frame.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3140. In B2B, Agents Narrow The Field—But The Human Still Buys

Rob Green is the chief digital officer of Insight Enterprises, a Fortune 500 Solutions Integrator.

Online B2C consumer behavior is often compared to spearfishing: targeting a specific product for optimal pricing, fulfillment and customer service. Consumers know what they want, find it, possibly add unplanned items to their carts based on cross-sell recommendations and hit the buy button. It’s the opposite of casting a wide net.

However, that’s consumer behavior. John Dawes’ 95:5 rule holds that only 5% of B2B buyers are spearfishing for specific solutions in a given quarter. They buy in bulk for a specific use case or a specific persona, with a range of purchase criteria.

Both buyers are about to offload that work to an agent, and the difference in what they’re offloading matters.

With AI-enabled buying entering the mainstream, will B2C and B2B agentic buying trends mirror e-commerce trends of the past 20 years? It’s hard to say because most everything we’ve heard about agentic buying is biased toward B2C buyers.

Meta’s Muse, launched in September, checks out for consumers at major retailers, but Amazon blocked it within two weeks. Every headline is about who gets to buy for the shopper. McKinsey’s own agentic commerce report targets consumers and merchants, but B2B only appears once in a parenthetical aside as a “significant” category its figures omit.

I don’t think it’s safe to assume B2B agentic buying will lag B2C, which means the B2B-specific effects of AI need your attention. It’s now in the top five channels B2B buyers rely on to find, research, short-list and evaluate suppliers. How do we adjust to a B2B buying journey that moves faster than the humans inside it?

A 2026 G2 report found that 51% of surveyed B2B software buyers began the purchasing process with AI rather than a traditional search engine, while a 2026 Gartner, Inc. survey found that two-thirds of responding B2B buyers preferred a rep-free experience outright.

Those two numbers reconcile once you understand which actions B2B buyers are asking AI to complete. According to IDC, “buyers now rely on these systems to narrow the field before human evaluation even begins.” Narrowing isn’t buying. B2B buyers are asking agents to do their research and deliver informed recommendations. They’re not asking AI to complete the purchase process (at least not yet).

B2B buyers seek informed, reliable and accurate responses to their specific use cases. We had education buyers who came to us and asked, “What’s the best laptop for a K-12 school system with 5,000 students?” The buyers expect an instantaneous answer that demonstrates judgment, expertise and an awareness of how the category has moved since they made their last sizable order of laptops.

The category keeps moving. What used to be a straightforward bulk laptop purchase is now significantly more complex. Am I going to move AI workloads onto these machines? If so, which laptop should I buy?

Notice that the role an AI agent plays in a B2B buying journey isn’t the one a salesperson plays. It’s a purchase advisor built to decomplexify. Its job is helping a buyer stay up to speed in a category that keeps moving faster than anyone tracking it part-time can follow, and buyers are listening. The G2 report found that 69% of respondents said they chose a different vendor than the one they’d planned on based on what the chatbot told them.

What’s true of your GPS is also true of AI: The guidance is only as reliable as the data supporting it. One of the key insights we had when developing our procurement agent was that product data fidelity needs to be accurate and reliable. Absent precise product data, an agent makes poorly informed recommendations while sounding every bit as certain as a good one.

B2B buyers can’t afford to make poorly informed decisions. Forrester surveyed nearly 18,000 of them and found that answer engines “often deliver incomplete or unreliable information, creating mistrust” and that “buyers compensate for this by seeking validation from trusted sources.” According to McKinsey, inconsistent information from a supplier’s own teams is now the number one reason they switch suppliers, ahead of the inability to reach a knowledgeable representative.

In ideal conditions, B2C AI agents find the best option available, whereas a B2B AI agent identifies the best solution a B2B buyer is seeking within their buying constraints or guidelines. Buying guardrails include approved company standards, budget constraints, approval workflows and timing.

Procurement sets those rules up front, and the agent works within these constraints to support buying decisions. OpenAI’s dots, launched September 29, ship with rules for when the agent must ask for approval and when it may act, and its first enterprise pilots include procurement. Procurement professionals, which Forrester found to be “decision-makers in 53% of business buying cycles,” are that human in the loop.

1. Product data must be accurate and reliable. Audit your product data before launch day.

2. Encode buying rules as guardrails. Product data informs the agents of buying options. Ask yourself what the agent needs to know to support the buying decision.

3. Clarify the agent’s role for salespeople. If a sales team views an agent as a threat to their livelihood, they’ll be blockers of agentic buying, not promoters.

4. Track the questions it can’t answer. Keeping a running list of questions that go beyond the scope of what the agent was built to handle functions as an honest product roadmap.

The sales wisdom that says customers buy from people they “know, like and trust” hasn’t changed. The only difference now is that the advisor can be an agent, and it has the same trust to earn. Get the data right and the humans in the right place, and I believe B2B buyers will also come to know, like and trust your agent.​

(Full disclosure: Insight Enterprises has commercial relationships with Amazon Web Services, Meta and OpenAI.)

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3138. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

TL;DR: Rates fell today, but not by enough to change your mortgage math.

Mortgage rates fell today, possibly helped by post-midterm election clarity and a drop in oil prices.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 7.01% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is six basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Tomorrow, the Nerds will be watching the release of the October jobs report. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.51% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3135. What Does a ‘Blue Wave’ Mean for Mortgage Rates?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Democrats won big in the midterms, but mortgage rates have bigger forces to reckon with.

Mortgage rates fell again this week, extending a gradual slide that began well before Election Day.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage fell 13 basis points to 7.05% APR in the week ending Nov. 5, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

Homebuyers’ budgets are feeling the pinch from high mortgage rates, steep home prices and a shortage of listings. On the campaign trail, candidates from both parties promised to make homeownership more affordable. But solving that problem is like untangling 20 years’ worth of Christmas lights from your attic. Yank on one strand, and three more tighten.

“Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run and more housing supply plus a more dynamic labor market in the long run,” Jake Krimmel, senior economist for Realtor.com, said in an email.

A shift in political power won’t erase those challenges overnight. Here's what's driving mortgage rates right now — and where a Democratic Congress might have some pull.

How that affects mortgage rates: The relief of one less uncertainty is giving rates some breathing room.

Election results have cleared up one big unknown for investors. With Democrats controlling Congress and Republicans holding the White House, markets are bracing for the reality that any major spending bills could face roadblocks.

Political gridlock isn't always popular, but weirdly, investors sometimes welcome it. When a divided government limits new spending, the government may need to borrow less than expected — which could turn down the heat on mortgage rates.

But there’s a catch. That same gridlock could make it harder to pass laws to help bring down housing costs.

How that affects mortgage rates: Heavy borrowing from the government and tech companies adds upward pressure to the bond market, where mortgage rates take their cues.

When the government spends more than it collects in taxes, it borrows the difference by selling Treasury bonds. When there's lots of debt for sale, investors may demand higher returns, called yields. Mortgage rates tend to follow the yield on the 10-year Treasury note.

The new Congress still faces a mountain of government debt. What they choose to do about it can ripple through the economy to affect your monthly mortgage payment.

“Sound fiscal policy, rather than deficit-financed spending and standoffs over budgets and the debt limit, can help keep rates in check,” Andy Winkler, managing director for housing and infrastructure policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said in an email.

Meanwhile, Big Tech companies are borrowing a boatload of money to build AI data centers. To get the cash, they issue corporate bonds, giving investors another place to put their money. More bonds flooding the market can push Treasury yields higher, taking mortgage rates along for the ride.

But how long can the AI building boom last? Voters across party lines pushed back against data centers in the midterms. Even if lawmakers pump the brakes on new construction, a slowdown in tech spending doesn't guarantee cheaper mortgages.

How that affects mortgage rates: Wartime disruptions to global oil supplies can fuel inflation, pushing borrowing costs higher.

The unpopular war in Iran likely added to the voter frustration that helped fuel Democrats' midterm victories. Higher energy costs can hit household budgets in multiple ways: at the pump, through pricier goods that need to be shipped, and through higher interest rates on loans (including mortgages) as inflation heats up.

“A resolution would likely improve oil supply, helping to ease price pressures and potentially providing relief for mortgage rates,” Krimmel said.

A Democratic Congress could push for a different approach to ending the war, but it can't make Iran agree to a deal.

Meanwhile, stubborn inflation from the Iran war has raised the prospect of another Federal Reserve rate hike at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. Futures traders now put the odds of a quarter-point increase at more than 80%, according to CME FedWatch. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but investors anticipate its decisions — so mortgage rates often move before the Fed does.

If buying a home makes sense for your budget now, there's little reason to wait just because Congress changed hands. Buyers can sometimes score lower prices in winter, when sellers are more willing to negotiate. But with today’s mortgage rates hovering above 7%, higher borrowing costs can eat into those savings, so it’s important to compare mortgage lenders to get the best rate.

For longer-term hope, look to the bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which became law this summer. This legislation shows lawmakers can, in fact, work together to make meaningful progress on housing affordability.

“Even with divided government, lawmakers and the administration can build on that common ground to further boost housing production, preserve existing affordable homes, and help families struggling with high housing and utility costs,” Winkler said.

But new homes take time to build, and your house hunt doesn't have to follow Congress's calendar. Your homebuying budget and local market conditions matter more than the next political headline.

#3134. Banks are set to report this week. Here's what the options market expects

Bank earnings kick off this week with no fewer than seven of the biggest names set to report. With the S&P 500 near all-time highs, the results could provide a significant market catalyst.Financials are the third-worst performing S&P 500 sector in 2026. Despite being flat on the year, the options market is pricing in some big moves as investor weigh the impact of higher rates and stretched consumer.

Here's what the options market sees, according to the latest Cboe LiveVol data.Goldman Sachs - Tuesday, Oct. 13

The options market is pricing in a 2.84% move for Goldman Sachs after it reports earnings. Across the last eight reported quarters, the stock has moved just about 2% on average. #3132. Marriott Bonvoy Boundless vs. Bevy: Which One Comes Out On Top?

The right card for you may depend on where you travel, how often, and your level of elite status with Bonvoy.

Two of the popular credit cards in the Marriott Bonvoy portfolio deliver various benefits to their users. The right one for you may depend on your typical travel patterns and history, such as if you already have a high level of elite status with Marriott or you travel only to certain regions.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card both come with valuable perks, but there are quite a few considerations that are worth a thorough review. Many travelers prefer not to have two cards from the same loyalty program, and these two cards are actually quite different from each other (including being issued by different banks).

Let’s take a look at these two Marriott co-branded cards to see if one may serve your needs over the other.

Here's how the cards stack up on the basics.

Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card

While the “winning” card for some may not apply to everyone, we wanted to analyze the card that provides exceptional benefits for travelers looking for a Marriott card in exchange for the cost of the annual fee. Those new to Marriott will find this to be a solid entry-level card, but it also works for Marriott Bonvoy fans looking for some extra perks without a huge outlay of money for an annual fee. These are the benefits that handed this card the win.

The annual fee on the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is notably less at $95 compared with $250 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card. While the earning rates when using the card to pay for Marriott stays are nearly the same, and both have bonus categories where you can earn multiples of points based on everyday spending, the lower-annual-fee card offers a lot of points earning for its low annual fee.

On top of the lower annual fee, it comes with an automatic annual free night award, Silver Elite status and a fast path to achieve Gold if you use the card often. Add to that an annual $100 in airline credits and DoorDash DashPass membership, and you have more than covered the annual fee without considering the other perks.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card’s $250 annual fee is higher, and its annual free night award requires spending $15,000 in eligible purchases. But the card comes with automatic Gold Elite status and more bonus points earned.

There are other cards (like American Express Platinum Card®) that also include free Gold Elite status with Marriott that can provide decent value for their annual fee (enrollment required). From a pure cash maximization perspective, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card wins because it provides more bang for your buck.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is the only one of the two cards to include an annual free night award, and it has a lower annual fee. It can be used at properties up to 35,000 points a night. This can render great value and is more than worth the $95 annual fee. Hands down, it is the winner in this category.

If you spend more than $15,000 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card in a calendar year, you can unlock a free night certificate valid at up to 50,000 points. This is even more valuable than the included certificate that comes with the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, but it also requires you to spend more on this card to get it.

It is important to consider the opportunity cost of not putting certain types of spend on other cards where you could be earning greater multiples of points for the same type of spending. Would losing those points be worth the value of a one-night-free certificate? Since Marriott relies on dynamic pricing, which means that points rates for a redemption night can fluctuate regularly, in many cases the value may not reach the level of the missed points opportunity.

While both cards offer decent travel protection benefits, only the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card comes with a flight credit. You can earn up to $100 in statement credits when making purchases directly with airlines by spending $250 on the card before the end of 2026 for a $50 statement credit and another $50 credit by spending $50 before June 30, 2027. That gives another point to the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card Boundless card.

» Learn more: Should I be loyal to a single airline?

If you don’t already have a Marriott co-branded credit card and you want to diversify what’s in your wallet, this could be a great option for big spenders. And if you are looking for one of the fastest paths to high Marriott elite status, this card can help you get there.

Both the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card cards offer bonus points on spending, but the more expensive annual fee Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card offers more points on more spending opportunities, including the below:

18.5X points on Marriott Bonvoy property spending (1.5X more than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card card).

4X points at global restaurants, including U.S. takeout and delivery plus U.S. Supermarkets on up to $15,000 in combined purchases, then 2X points (that’s 1X more than what Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card offers and available on $9,000 more in spending).

1,000 bonus points when staying at a Marriott Bonvoy property.

Depending on how much you spend in these categories, you have the ability to earn more points with this card than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, including when you are not traveling. And then when you are, you earn 1,000 Marriott bonus points for each paid, eligible stay booked directly with Marriott. This gives it an edge, even though the annual fee is higher.

» Learn more: Hilton versus Marriott — Which one is right for you?

When you get the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, you’ll receive 15 elite night credits deposited into your Marriott account. This is on top of the instant Gold Elite status you earn with the card, which normally requires staying 25 nights. To reach the next tier, Platinum, you only need an additional 10 nights to reach its 50-night minimum.

This means not only do you start out at a higher elite tier with Marriott just for having this card, but also you can progress more quickly to Platinum. For frequent travelers, the best value of this card is the opportunity to reach the next status tier faster. And if you wanted to keep going, you would only need 35 more nights to get to Titanium, which is rich with on-property perks like lounge access, welcome gifts and room upgrades.

Earning these nights within a calendar year is not difficult for Marriott loyalists, especially if you hit the jackpot during a promotion that offers double elite nights.

The status perks don’t stop there. Once you hit Marriott’s Titanium status, you can then link your account with United Airlines to earn United’s Premier Silver elite status for more benefits when you fly United, including greater baggage allowance and free extra-legroom economy seats at check-in.

» Learn more: Marriott elite status: what you need to know

Both of these cards provide Marriott Bonvoy points earning and elite status, and they both offer a great way to expedite points earning or receive travel and purchase protections when using the card. But the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card packs a greater punch in its benefits for a lower annual fee. It becomes a great additional card to others you may have (like the American Express Platinum Card®, which has its own impressive travel perk lineup).

If you are a Marriott frequent guest, the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card offers higher-tier Gold status and a faster path to even better tiers for perks. But, it costs more each year and does not offer a free annual night.

Deciding which perks are more important for you, as well as looking at what benefits come from other cards in your wallet, will help you make an informed decision on which of the two best suits your needs.

For rates and fees of the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, please visit American Express' rates and fees page.

#3130. Breakfast News: Staying Calm in a Correction

Where our analysts see opportunity in the AI sell-off.

When stocks fall this fast, it’s tempting to assume they’ll keep falling, and acting on that fear is often costly.

Three of our analysts offer a more measured view of the AI sell-off, each through their team’s philosophy. Yasser El-Shimy and Sanmeet Deo bring the Rule Breakers lens of patience with bold, long-term bets, while Matt Frankel brings Hidden Gems’ disciplined but flexible approach to putting cash to work. All three also share stocks on their radar right now.

Read on for how each of them is approaching the decline!

Yesterday felt like free fall: the Nasdaq Composite sank nearly 5%, erasing Wednesday’s post-election rally and putting the index on pace to close in correction territory, as Nvidia (NVDA -9.26%) and Micron (MU -17.46%) led a broad AI/chip sell-off.

As David Gardner, our Chief Rule Breaker, likes to say: “The stock market always goes down faster than it goes up, but it always goes up more than it goes down.” For Rule Breakers, the only term that counts is the long-term. Yes, political scrutiny may crimp AI capex growth. But a slower build-out likely extends the cycle rather than compressing it into three or four manic years — bullish, in my view, for patient investors willing to buy into the irrational selling likely over the coming days and weeks. This is a marathon, not a sprint.

One stock I’m eyeing after the sell-off is Shopify (SHOP -6.06%), whose price chart says something broke even though the business numbers say otherwise. Shopify fell about 8% after posting $119 billion in GMV, up 29%, revenue up 32% to $3.7 billion, and monthly recurring revenue up 19%, then slid further as today’s tech selloff dragged every highly-valued stock down with it. Buried under the carnage are signs of a resilient business that thrives in an AGI era: AI-driven traffic and orders to merchant storefronts tripled year over year, and Shopify is quietly becoming checkout infrastructure for the agentic web.

This is why Meta Platforms (META -3.96%) partnered with the Canadian company to power Muse AI’s e-commerce infrastructure. Of course, a stock near 70 times earnings leaves little room for error. But I’d call this a valuation reset, not a verdict on execution. I welcome further price declines on this business, because the last quarter makes me want to add more to my already sizable position.

It has been a rough few weeks in the stock market, especially when it comes to AI-related stocks like chipmakers and infrastructure names. The Nasdaq is now in correction territory, falling 10% from its October high, and in many cases the selling has little to do with how well the underlying businesses are doing, and is driven by fears related to the AI build-out and how it's being paid for.

Of course, nobody knows where the bottom is. It's entirely possible that stocks could fall further before things get better. But for long-term investors, paying lower prices means paying less for the future earnings of solid businesses, and there are some excellent businesses that trade for valuations we haven't seen in a while.

On Team Hidden Gems, we try to stay disciplined but flexible, and sell-offs like this one are when that approach pays off. We keep some dry powder on hand and watch our Potential Growth Indicator (PGI), which compares the cash sitting in money market accounts with the total value of U.S. stocks. When fearful investors retreat to cash, the PGI rises, and we get more eager to buy great businesses at lower prices.

One AI infrastructure play in particular I'm looking at is Comfort Systems (FIX -11.58%) which makes HVAC systems for commercial buildings, particularly data centers. Its backlog nearly doubled year-over-year in the third quarter, but the stock has fallen more than 50% from its peak and trades for less than 17 times forward earnings, compared with its five-year average of 26.

Another on my radar is Alphabet (GOOGL -2.20%)(GOOG -1.93%), which had its worst day in a long time after it reported recent earnings, even though Google Cloud's revenue more than doubled and overall revenue grew by nearly 25%. Concerns about Google's advertising results are fair and are worth watching, but at the current price, Alphabet trades for less than 15 times training earnings, far below other mega-cap tech companies.

It’s been an unsettling roller coaster in the stock market the past few days and your emotions may be running hot. This volatility is the price of admission to ride what we call investing. An important aspect of investing I have learned in my career is patience. If I lacked it, I missed out on some very big winners and was left with regret. Warren Buffet once said that “The stock market is designed to transfer money from the impatient to the patient.” Have you initiated that transfer or are you managing your emotions? A good first step would be to take a couple minutes to take long exhale breaths to calm the nervous system. Then, you trust the habits and mindsets you have built to make patience natural.

The Rule Breaker Investing recipe for patience starts before you buy: commit to holding at least three years, so inevitable drops don’t trigger fear. Put on the jersey and stick with companies you love, like fans cheering through a bad season. Expect a rollercoaster, but remember this ride ends on a mountaintop, with the market’s roughly 10% long-run return at your back. Ignore minute-by-minute wiggles. Be a winter soldier, not a sunshine patriot who flees at the first storm. And lean on structure over willpower: own plenty of stocks, size positions to your sleep number, buy in thirds, and review quarterly, not daily.

Owning plenty of stocks also means looking beyond AI. SharkNinja (SN -0.97%), which just reported earnings, is top of mind for me right now. It delivered excellent sales growth, surging net income, and expanding gross margins, and it sells products customers use daily that won’t be affected by AI, data centers, or capex spend. While it won’t completely soothe the red in our portfolios, it’s a reminder to make sure our portfolios consist of a diversified group of companies that reduce exposure to one hot sector.

And in the ultimate power move to face your fears, find a stock you have wanted to own for a while and just buy a few shares (nothing crazy) and let it ride. Now the impatient investor has transferred money to you. You’ll feel better that you were able to zig while the market zags.

Which stocks from your watchlist are you eyeing now that valuations have pulled back?

#3128. Arlo’s Third Quarter: A Good Story, Priced on 1 Launch

Arlo's near-two-thirds recurring mix and 49% non-GAAP gross margin show why the subscription engine matters more than hardware sales.

On Nov. 5, Arlo Technologies (ARLO -2.50%) told investors that its security cameras are becoming a subscription business. That is mostly true. The harder question is whether the next leg of growth is as certain as management made it sound.

Arlo sells cameras at a loss on purpose, then collects monthly fees at high margins. Subscription and services revenue hit a record $96.0 million, up 20%, and made up 64.4% of revenue, with non-GAAP services gross margin of 83.9%. GAAP, or Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, is the official accounting standard. Non-GAAP is management's adjusted version. Paid accounts reached 6.6 million, up 23%.

That is a real, durable machine. The call's three claims, though, each come with a footnote.

ARR, or Annual Recurring Revenue, is subscription revenue annualized. It is $369.9 million, growing 14.5%. The 2027 target requires doubling that growth rate.

The bridge is Comcast, ADT, and a new elder-care business. Comcast is the big one. Management has framed its 31 million broadband households as a potential opportunity comparable to Arlo's European alarm partner, Verisure. But the launch has already slipped toward early 2027, subject to field testing.

The filings add a wrinkle. Paid accounts are growing faster than ARR, so revenue per account has fallen roughly 7% in a year, by this analysis. More customers are paying less each. Quarterly ARR additions also shrank, from about $8 million in Q2 to about $5 million in Q3.

The share is accurate, but part of the reason is that hardware shrank. Product revenue fell from $59.6 million to $53.1 million while total revenue grew about 7%. Services gross margin also slipped, from 85.1% to 83.9% on the non-GAAP basis. The call offered no explanation.

Profit. GAAP operating income was $1.0 million on $149 million of revenue. Gross profit rose $12.9 million year over year, but operating expenses rose $12.7 million. The gain was absorbed.

The main culprit is stock-based compensation, or stock paid to employees instead of cash. It was $21.7 million in the quarter versus $13.1 million a year ago. Over nine months, the company repurchased $44.4 million of stock and still saw shares outstanding rise from 105.0 million to 107.5 million. A new $100 million buyback was authorized anyway. Free cash flow of $50.6 million over nine months falls below zero once $63.2 million of stock comp is subtracted.

One more item to watch. Channel inventory at U.S. retailers rose to 14.6 weeks from 9.6, and at U.S. distributors to 14.9 weeks from 5.9. Q4 revenue guidance of $137 million to $147 million sits below Q3.

The investment thesis survives, but it narrows. Arlo has no debt and $144.0 million in cash and short-term investments, so the downside is cushioned. The upside, however, rests largely on one partner launch arriving on time and on revenue per account stabilizing.

At roughly $13 before the print, about 3.4 times ARR on enterprise value, the stock is fairly priced if growth stays in the mid-teens and attractive only if the 30% target proves real. Three signals matter most: the Comcast launch date, ARR per account, and whether buybacks finally shrink the share count.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Arlo Technologies. The Motley Fool recommends Comcast. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3127. 2027 Tax Brackets and Federal Income Tax Rates

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Each year, the IRS adjusts tax brackets to keep pace with inflation. The 2027 tax brackets were announced on Nov. 5, 2026.

The IRS has released updated tax brackets for 2027, which apply to the taxes we file in 2028. You may notice that the seven tax rates generally stay the same year-to-year — so why do tax brackets get adjusted?

Well, due to inflation, our money might not stretch as far as it did the previous year. To combat that, the IRS adjusts tax brackets so taxpayers can keep a little bit more of their money in a lower tax bracket compared with the previous year.

For example, a single filer could make up to $50,400 in 2026 and pay a top tax rate of 12%. But in 2027, they could make up to $52,025 and still only pay a top tax rate of 12% thanks to these inflation adjustments.

Not sure how to read the tax bracket tables below?

Find your total taxable income in the "tax bracket" column. The corresponding tax rate is the highest rate you'll pay — it doesn’t apply to all your income; it applies only to the portion that falls within that bracket. The income in the lower brackets is taxed at those lower rates.

For example, let's say you’re a single filer and you expect to earn $150,000 this year. The top rate you’d pay on a portion of your income is 24%. The rest would be taxed at 10%, 12% and 22%.
